Final SQL:
```
WITH date_jun AS (...),
date_jun_aug AS (...),
store_sales_agg AS (
    SELECT
        ss.ss_customer_sk,
        ss.ss_item_sk,
        ss.ss_ticket_number,
        ss.ss_store_sk,
        MIN(ss.ss_net_profit) AS min_ss_net_profit
    FROM store_sales ss
    JOIN date_jun d1 ON d1.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
    GROUP BY ss.ss_customer_sk, ss.ss_item_sk, ss.ss_ticket_number, ss.ss_store_sk
),
store_returns_agg AS (
    SELECT
        sr.sr_customer_sk,
        sr.sr_item_sk,
        sr.sr_ticket_number,
        MIN(sr.sr_net_loss) AS min_sr_net_loss
    FROM store_returns sr
    JOIN date_jun_aug d2 ON d2.d_date_sk = sr.sr_returned_date_sk
    GROUP BY sr.sr_customer_sk, sr.sr_item_sk, sr.sr_ticket_number
),
catalog_sales_agg AS (
    SELECT
        cs.cs_bill_customer_sk,
        cs.cs_item_sk,
        MIN(cs.cs_net_profit) AS min_cs_net_profit
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN date_jun_aug d3 ON d3.d_date_sk = cs.cs_sold_date_sk
    GROUP BY cs.cs_bill_customer_sk, cs.cs_item_sk
),
joined_agg AS (
    SELECT
        ss.ss_item_sk,
        ss.ss_store_sk,
        MIN(ss.min_ss_net_profit) AS store_sales_profit,
        MIN(sr.min_sr_net_loss) AS store_returns_loss,
        MIN(cs.min_cs_net_profit) AS catalog_sales_profit
    FROM store_sales_agg ss
    JOIN store_returns_agg sr
      ON sr.sr_customer_sk = ss.ss_customer_sk
     AND sr.sr_item_sk = ss.ss_item_sk
     AND sr.sr_ticket_number = ss.ss_ticket_number
    JOIN catalog_sales_agg cs
      ON cs.cs_bill_customer_sk = sr.sr_customer_sk
     AND cs.cs_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
    GROUP BY ss.ss_item_sk, ss.ss_store_sk
)
SELECT
    i.i_item_id,
    i.i_item_desc,
    s.s_store_id,
    s.s_store_name,
    joined_agg.store_sales_profit,
    joined_agg.store_returns_loss,
    joined_agg.catalog_sales_profit
FROM joined_agg
JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = joined_agg.ss_item_sk
JOIN store s ON s.s_store_sk = joined_agg.ss_store_sk
ORDER BY i.i_item_id, i.i_item_desc, s.s_store_id, s.s_store_name
LIMIT 100;
```

```sql
select i_item_id, i_item_desc, s_store_id, s_store_name,
       min(ss_net_profit) as store_sales_profit,
       min(sr_net_loss) as store_returns_loss,
       min(cs_net_profit) as catalog_sales_profit
from store_sales ss
join store_returns sr
  on ss.ss_customer_sk = sr.sr_customer_sk
 and ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
 and ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
join catalog_sales cs
  on sr.sr_customer_sk = cs.cs_bill_customer_sk
 and sr.sr_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
join date_dim d1 on d1.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
join date_dim d2 on d2.d_date_sk = sr.sr_returned_date_sk
join date_dim d3 on d3.d_date_sk = cs.cs_sold_date_sk
join store s on s.s_store_sk = ss.ss_store_sk
join item i on i.i_item_sk = ss.ss_item_sk
where d1.d_moy = 6 and d1.d_year = 2002
  and d2.d_moy between 6 and 8 and d2.d_year = 2002
  and d3.d_moy between 6 and 8 and d

=== Worker 1: Start Rewrite ===

</produced_sql>
```sql
WITH date_jun AS (
    SELECT d_date_sk
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002
      AND d_moy = 6
),
date_jun_aug AS (
    SELECT d_date_sk
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002
      AND d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8
),
store_sales_agg AS (
    SELECT ss.ss_customer_sk,
           ss.ss_item_sk,
           ss.ss_ticket_number,
           ss.ss_store_sk,
           MIN(ss.ss_net_profit) AS min_ss_net_profit
    FROM store_sales ss
    JOIN date_jun d1 ON d1.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
    GROUP BY ss.ss_customer_sk, ss.ss_item_sk, ss.ss_ticket_number, ss.ss_store_sk
),
store_returns_agg AS (
    SELECT sr.sr_customer_sk,
           sr.sr_item_sk,
           sr.sr_ticket_number,
           MIN(sr.sr_net_loss) AS min_sr_net_loss
    FROM store_returns sr
    JOIN date_jun_aug d2 ON d2.d_date_sk = sr.sr_returned_date_sk
    GROUP BY sr.sr_customer_sk, sr.sr_item_sk, sr.sr_ticket_number
),
catalog_sales_agg AS (
    SELECT cs.cs_bill_customer_sk,
           cs.cs_item_sk,
           MIN(cs.cs_net_profit) AS min_cs_net_profit
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN date_jun_aug d3 ON d3.d_date_sk = cs.cs_sold_date_sk
    GROUP BY cs.cs_bill_customer_sk, cs.cs_item_sk
),
joined_agg AS (
    SELECT ss.ss_item_sk,
           ss.ss_store_sk,
           MIN(ss.min_ss_net_profit) AS store_sales_profit,
           MIN(sr.min_sr_net_loss) AS store_returns_loss,
           MIN(cs.min_cs_net_profit) AS catalog_sales_profit
    FROM store_sales_agg ss
    JOIN store_returns_agg sr
      ON sr.sr_customer_sk = ss.ss_customer_sk
     AND sr.sr_item_sk = ss.ss_item_sk
     AND sr.sr_ticket_number = ss.ss_ticket_number
    JOIN catalog_sales_agg cs
      ON cs.cs_bill_customer_sk = sr.sr_customer_sk
     AND cs.cs_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
    GROUP BY ss.ss_item_sk, ss.ss_store_sk
)
SELECT i.i_item_id,
       i.i_item_desc,
       s.s_store_id,
       s.s_store_name,
       j.store_sales_profit,
       j.store_returns_loss,
       j.catalog_sales_profit
FROM joined_agg j
JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = j.ss_item_sk
JOIN store s ON s.s_store_sk = j.ss_store_sk
ORDER BY i.i_item_id, i.i_item_desc, s.s_store_id, s.s_store_name
LIMIT 100;
```

</analysis>
The original query joins three large fact tables (28.8M store_sales, 7.2M store_returns, 7.2M catalog_sales) before computing MIN aggregates, causing row explosion. Since MIN is distributive over one-to-many joins when each fact stream is pre-aggregated on its join keys, each fact table can be reduced first (filtered by date CTE, then grouped by customer/item/ticket/store keys). The final aggregation groups by integer surrogate keys (ss_item_sk, ss_store_sk) which uniquely determine i_item_id/i_item_desc and s_store_id/s_store_name in TPC-DS, allowing dimension joins to be deferred until after aggregation. Optional supporting indexes: date_dim(d_year, d_moy, d_date_sk); store_sales(ss_sold_date_sk, ss_customer_sk, ss_item_sk, ss_ticket_number, ss_store_sk) INCLUDE (ss_net_profit); store_returns(sr_returned_date_sk, sr_customer_sk, sr_item_sk, sr_ticket_number) INCLUDE (sr_net_loss); catalog_sales(cs_sold_date_sk, cs_bill_customer_sk, cs_item_sk) INCLUDE (cs_net_profit).
</analysis>

WITH date_jun AS (
    SELECT d_date_sk
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002
      AND d_moy = 6
),
date_jun_aug AS (
    SELECT d_date_sk
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2002
      AND d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8
),
store_sales_agg AS (
    SELECT ss.ss_customer_sk,
           ss.ss_item_sk,
           ss.ss_ticket_number,
           ss.ss_store_sk,
           MIN(ss.ss_net_profit) AS min_ss_net_profit
    FROM store_sales ss
    JOIN date_jun d1 ON d1.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
    GROUP BY ss.ss_customer_sk, ss.ss_item_sk, ss.ss_ticket_number, ss.ss_store_sk
),
store_returns_agg AS (
    SELECT sr.sr_customer_sk,
           sr.sr_item_sk,
           sr.sr_ticket_number,
           MIN(sr.sr_net_loss) AS min_sr_net_loss
    FROM store_returns sr
    JOIN date_jun_aug d2 ON d2.d_date_sk = sr.sr_returned_date_sk
    GROUP BY sr.sr_customer_sk, sr.sr_item_sk, sr.sr_ticket_number
),
catalog_sales_agg AS (
    SELECT cs.cs_bill_customer_sk,
           cs.cs_item_sk,
           MIN(cs.cs_net_profit) AS min_cs_net_profit
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN date_jun_aug d3 ON d3.d_date_sk = cs.cs_sold_date_sk
    GROUP BY cs.cs_bill_customer_sk, cs.cs_item_sk
),
joined_agg AS (
    SELECT ss.ss_item_sk,
           ss.ss_store_sk,
           MIN(ss.min_ss_net_profit) AS store_sales_profit,
           MIN(sr.min_sr_net_loss) AS store_returns_loss,
           MIN(cs.min_cs_net_profit) AS catalog_sales_profit
    FROM store_sales_agg ss
    JOIN store_returns_agg sr
      ON sr.sr_customer_sk = ss.ss_customer_sk
     AND sr.sr_item_sk = ss.ss_item_sk
     AND sr.sr_ticket_number = ss.ss_ticket_number
    JOIN catalog_sales_agg cs
      ON cs.cs_bill_customer_sk = sr.sr_customer_sk
     AND cs.cs_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
    GROUP BY ss.ss_item_sk, ss.ss_store_sk
)
SELECT i.i_item_id,
       i.i_item_desc,
       s.s_store_id,
       s.s_store_name,
       j.store_sales_profit,
       j.store_returns_loss,
       j.catalog_sales_profit
FROM joined_agg j
JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = j.ss_item_sk
JOIN store s ON s.s_store_sk = j.ss_store_sk
ORDER BY i.i_item_id, i.i_item_desc, s.s_store_id, s.s_store_name
LIMIT 100;

A careful line-by-line comparison of the three EXPLAIN outputs reveals the following:

1. Structural identity: All three plans share exactly the same operator tree — the same join order, the same access methods (Parallel Index Scan on date_dim d2, Index Scans on store_returns/store_sales/store/item, BitmapAnd over two Bitmap Index Scans on catalog_sales, plus Index Scans on date_dim d1/d3), the same Gather Merge with 2 workers, and the same top-level Sort + GroupAggregate + Limit stack.

2. Cost identity: Total plan cost is 35241.42..35253.68 in the original and 35241.42..35253.67 in both rewritten and enhanced plans — a difference of 0.01 cost units (~0.00003%), which is purely rounding noise, not a real optimization.

3. The only substantive textual difference: The original plan carried an explicit residual `Join Filter: (catalog_sales.cs_item_sk = item.i_item_sk)` on the upper Nested Loop at cost 35240.83. In the rewritten and enhanced plans this filter no longer appears, because the rewrite pushed the cs_item_sk predicate directly into the Bitmap Index Scan on idx_catalog_sales_item (Index Cond: cs_item_sk = …). This means the condition is now enforced inside the index access path rather than as a post-join residual filter — a semantically cleaner placement, though the optimizer costs the node identically.

4. Alias rewriting: The rewritten and enhanced plans introduce short aliases (i, s, sr, ss, cs) and reference them consistently in join conditions. This is a readability/robustness improvement only; it has no effect on costing.

5. No differences between rewritten and enhanced plans: The two are byte-for-byte identical in structure and cost, indicating the enhancement step produced no additional optimizer-recognizable gains for this query.
</analysis>

</report>
Cost Efficiency:
- Overall cost change percentage: The total estimated cost changed from 35253.68 (original) to 35253.67 (rewritten/enhanced), a reduction of approximately 0.00003% — effectively negligible/zero. The startup cost (35241.42) is unchanged across all three plans.
- Cost change at the most expensive plan node: The most expensive node in all three plans is the innermost Nested Loop over date_dim d2 → store_returns → store_sales (cost 0.72..16940.85, rows=3650), and its cost is identical in all three plans (0% change). The upper Nested Loop that previously carried a residual Join Filter is costed at 35240.83..35244.98 in all plans, showing the moved predicate did not alter the optimizer's estimates.

Plan Characteristics:
- Scan type transitions: None. All three plans already use index-based access everywhere — a Parallel Index Scan on idx_date_dim_sk, plain Index Scans on store_returns, store_sales (primary key), store, item, and date_dim (d1, d3), and a Bitmap Heap Scan on catalog_sales fed by a BitmapAnd of two Bitmap Index Scans. No Seq Scan appears in any plan, and the rewrite did not (and did not need to) change any access method.
- Join algorithm refinements: No join algorithm change. All plans rely exclusively on Nested Loop joins plus one Gather Merge to combine parallel worker output. The join order and join tree shape are identical across all three plans.
- Elimination of explicit sorting / intermediate result reduction: The per-worker Sort node remains in all three plans (Sort Key: i_item_id, i_item_desc, s_store_id, s_store_name) and cannot be eliminated because the GroupAggregate requires sorted input. However, the rewrite did eliminate one redundant evaluation point: the original plan enforced `cs_item_sk = item.i_item_sk` as a post-join residual Join Filter on the outer Nested Loop, while the rewritten/enhanced plans fold this predicate into the Bitmap Index Scan on idx_catalog_sales_item. This removes a redundant filter application per joined row and simplifies the join node, even though the estimated row count and cost stay the same.

Resource Utilization:
- Memory usage: No change. The single Sort node, the BitmapAnd/Bitmap Index Scan structures, and the width of intermediate rows (width=176 at the parallel branch, narrowing to 158 at the top) are identical across all plans. There are no Hash nodes in any plan, so no hash memory considerations apply.
- Worker count adjustment: Workers Planned: 2 in all three plans — no parallelism tuning was needed or applied.

Other Improvements:
- Predicate placement: The most meaningful (though cost-neutral) improvement is relocating the cs_item_sk join condition from a residual Join Filter into the index condition of the catalog_sales Bitmap Index Scan. This is a plan-quality improvement in principle: the predicate is enforced at the index-access layer, reducing per-row filter work on the join node, even though the optimizer's cost model assigns it zero incremental benefit here.
- Readability and maintainability: The rewritten and enhanced plans introduce concise, consistent table aliases (i, s, sr, ss, cs) and use them throughout all join conditions. This does not affect execution, but it makes the plan easier to read and reduces the chance of ambiguity in future query maintenance.
- Plan stability: The fact that the rewritten and enhanced plans are identical in both shape and cost confirms that the original query was already well-formed for this schema — appropriate indexes exist on every join/filter path, the date-dimension-driven driving order is optimal, and parallelism is correctly leveraged. The rewrite/validation cycle therefore serves primarily as a confirmation of plan optimality rather than as a source of performance gains.

Conclusion: This query is already near-optimally planned. The rewrite delivered only cosmetic and structural hygiene improvements (predicate pushdown into the index condition, alias normalization) with no measurable cost reduction. Remaining optimization opportunities would lie outside the current plan shape — e.g., composite indexing on date_dim (d_year, d_moy) to tighten the ~38-row date filter, or materialized view strategies — rather than in further plan rewriting.
</report>
</analysis>
A careful line-by-line comparison of the three EXPLAIN outputs reveals the following:

1. Structural identity: All three plans share exactly the same operator tree — the same join order, the same access methods (Parallel Index Scan on date_dim d2, Index Scans on store_returns/store_sales/store/item, BitmapAnd over two Bitmap Index Scans on catalog_sales, plus Index Scans on date_dim d1/d3), the same Gather Merge with 2 workers, and the same top-level Sort + GroupAggregate + Limit stack.

2. Cost identity: Total plan cost is 35241.42..35253.68 in the original and 35241.42..35253.67 in both rewritten and enhanced plans — a difference of 0.01 cost units (~0.00003%), which is purely rounding noise, not a real optimization.

3. The only substantive textual difference: The original plan carried an explicit residual `Join Filter: (catalog_sales.cs_item_sk = item.i_item_sk)` on the upper Nested Loop at cost 35240.83. In the rewritten and enhanced plans this filter no longer appears, because the rewrite pushed the cs_item_sk predicate directly into the Bitmap Index Scan on idx_catalog_sales_item (Index Cond: cs_item_sk = …). This means the condition is now enforced inside the index access path rather than as a post-join residual filter — a semantically cleaner placement, though the optimizer costs the node identically.

4. Alias rewriting: The rewritten and enhanced plans introduce short aliases (i, s, sr, ss, cs) and reference them consistently in join conditions. This is a readability/robustness improvement only; it has no effect on costing.

5. No differences between rewritten and enhanced plans: The two are byte-for-byte identical in structure and cost, indicating the enhancement step produced no additional optimizer-recognizable gains for this query.
</analysis>

</report>
Cost Efficiency:
- Overall cost change percentage: The total estimated cost changed from 35253.68 (original) to 35253.67 (rewritten/enhanced), a reduction of approximately 0.00003% — effectively negligible/zero. The startup cost (35241.42) is unchanged across all three plans.
- Cost change at the most expensive plan node: The most expensive node in all three plans is the innermost Nested Loop over date_dim d2 → store_returns → store_sales (cost 0.72..16940.85, rows=3650), and its cost is identical in all three plans (0% change). The upper Nested Loop that previously carried a residual Join Filter is costed at 35240.83..35244.98 in all plans, showing the moved predicate did not alter the optimizer's estimates.

Plan Characteristics:
- Scan type transitions: None. All three plans already use index-based access everywhere — a Parallel Index Scan on idx_date_dim_sk, plain Index Scans on store_returns, store_sales (primary key), store, item, and date_dim (d1, d3), and a Bitmap Heap Scan on catalog_sales fed by a BitmapAnd of two Bitmap Index Scans. No Seq Scan appears in any plan, and the rewrite did not (and did not need to) change any access method.
- Join algorithm refinements: No join algorithm change. All plans rely exclusively on Nested Loop joins plus one Gather Merge to combine parallel worker output. The join order and join tree shape are identical across all three plans.
- Elimination of explicit sorting / intermediate result reduction: The per-worker Sort node remains in all three plans (Sort Key: i_item_id, i_item_desc, s_store_id, s_store_name) and cannot be eliminated because the GroupAggregate requires sorted input. However, the rewrite did eliminate one redundant evaluation point: the original plan enforced `cs_item_sk = item.i_item_sk` as a post-join residual Join Filter on the outer Nested Loop, while the rewritten/enhanced plans fold this predicate into the Bitmap Index Scan on idx_catalog_sales_item. This removes a redundant filter application per joined row and simplifies the join node, even though the estimated row count and cost stay the same.

Resource Utilization:
- Memory usage: No change. The single Sort node, the BitmapAnd/Bitmap Index Scan structures, and the width of intermediate rows (width=176 at the parallel branch, narrowing to 158 at the top) are identical across all plans. There are no Hash nodes in any plan, so no hash memory considerations apply.
- Worker count adjustment: Workers Planned: 2 in all three plans — no parallelism tuning was needed or applied.

Other Improvements:
- Predicate placement: The most meaningful (though cost-neutral) improvement is relocating the cs_item_sk join condition from a residual Join Filter into the index condition of the catalog_sales Bitmap Index Scan. This is a plan-quality improvement in principle: the predicate is enforced at the index-access layer, reducing per-row filter work on the join node, even though the optimizer's cost model assigns it zero incremental benefit here.
- Readability and maintainability: The rewritten and enhanced plans introduce concise, consistent table aliases (i, s, sr, ss, cs) and use them throughout all join conditions. This does not affect execution, but it makes the plan easier to read and reduces the chance of ambiguity in future query maintenance.
- Plan stability: The fact that the rewritten and enhanced plans are identical in both shape and cost confirms that the original query was already well-formed for this schema — appropriate indexes exist on every join/filter path, the date-dimension-driven driving order is optimal, and parallelism is correctly leveraged. The rewrite/validation cycle therefore serves primarily as a confirmation of plan optimality rather than as a source of performance gains.

Conclusion: This query is already near-optimally planned. The rewrite delivered only cosmetic and structural hygiene improvements (predicate pushdown into the index condition, alias normalization) with no measurable cost reduction. Remaining optimization opportunities would lie outside the current plan shape — e.g., composite indexing on date_dim (d_year, d_moy) to tighten the ~38-row date filter, or materialized view strategies — rather than in further plan rewriting.
</report>

 as store_returns_loss,
       min(cs_net_profit) as catalog_sales_profit
from store_sales ss
join store_returns sr
  on ss.ss_customer_sk = sr.sr_customer_sk
 and ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
 and ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
join catalog_sales cs
  on sr.sr_customer_sk = cs.cs_bill_customer_sk
 and sr.sr_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
join date_dim d1 on d1.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
join date_dim d2 on d2.d_date_sk = sr.sr_returned_date_sk
join date_dim d3 on d3.d_date_sk = cs.cs_sold_date_sk
join store s on s.s_store_sk = ss.ss_store_sk
join item i on i.i_item_sk = ss.ss_item_sk
where d1.d_moy = 6
  and d1.d_year = 2002
  and d2.d_moy between 6 and 8
  and d2.d_year = 2002
  and d3.d_moy between 6 and 8
  and d3.d_year = 2002
group by i_item_id, i_item_desc, s_store_id, s_store_name
order by i_item_id, i_item_desc, s_store_id, s_store_name
limit 100;

Statement 0 embeds date_dim filter predicates (d_moy, d_year) directly in the JOIN ON clauses, while Statement 1 uses table aliases and keeps join conditions in ON clauses with filter predicates in the WHERE clause. Statement 1 is also cleaner and more maintainable with explicit aliases, reducing ambiguity. ## Selected Rewritten SQL: select i_item_id, i_item_desc, s_store_id, s_store_name,
       min(ss_net_profit) as store_sales_profit,
       min(sr_net_loss) as store_returns_loss,
       min(cs_net_profit) as catalog_sales_profit
from store_sales ss
join store_returns sr
  on ss.ss_customer_sk = sr.sr_customer_sk
 and ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
 and ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
join catalog_sales cs
  on sr.sr_customer_sk = cs.cs_bill_customer_sk
 and sr.sr_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
join date_dim d1 on d1.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
join date_dim d2 on d2.d_date_sk = sr.sr_returned_date_sk
join date_dim d3 on d3.d_date_sk = cs.cs_sold_date_sk
join store s on s.s_store_sk = ss.ss_store_sk
join item i on i.i_item_sk = ss.ss_item_sk
where d1.d_moy = 6
  and d1.d_year = 2002
  and d2.d_moy between 6 and 8
  and d2.d_year = 2002
  and d3.d_moy between 6 and 8
  and d3.d_year = 2002
group by i_item_id, i_item_desc, s_store_id, s_store_name
order by i_item_id, i_item_desc, s_store_id, s_store_name
limit 100; ##

For this multi-table query joining store_sales, store_returns, catalog_sales, three date_dim aliases, store, and item, using explicit JOIN ... ON clauses makes each join relationship clear and unambiguous. Key benefits: (1) better readability and maintainability in complex queries, (2) avoids accidental Cartesian products that the old comma-style can introduce when a join condition is missed, (3) prevents errors caused by mixing old and new join styles, and (4) avoids forcing the optimizer to internally rewrite old-style queries. No plan change is expected since both forms are semantically equivalent, but explicit JOINs reduce mistakes. Example rewrite pattern: SELECT * FROM people p JOIN companies c ON p.companyID = c.id WHERE p.firstName = 'Daniel'."

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
select i.i_item_id, i.i_item_desc, s.s_store_id, s.s_store_name,
       min(ss.ss_net_profit) as store_sales_profit,
       min(sr.sr_net_loss) as store_returns_loss,
       min(cs.cs_net_profit) as catalog_sales_profit
from store_sales ss
join store_returns sr
  on ss.ss_customer_sk = sr.sr_customer_sk
 and ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
 and ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
join catalog_sales cs
  on sr.sr_customer_sk = cs.cs_bill_customer_sk
 and sr.sr_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
join date_dim d1 on d1.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
join date_dim d2 on d2.d_date_sk = sr.sr_returned_date_sk
join date_dim d3 on d3.d_date_sk = cs.cs_sold_date_sk
join store s on s.s_store_sk = ss.ss_store_sk
join item i on i.i_item_sk = ss.ss_item_sk
where d1.d_moy = 6
  and d1.d_year = 2002
  and d2.d_moy between 6 and 8
  and d2.d_year = 2002
  and d3.d_moy between 6 and 8
  and d3.d_year = 2002
group by i.i_item_id, i.i_item_desc, s.s_store_id, s.s_store_name
order by i.i_item_id, i.i_item_desc, s.s_store_id, s.s_store_name
limit 100;
```

</analysis>
Both rewritten queries convert the original comma-join style into explicit ANSI INNER JOINs, which is semantically equivalent and helps optimizers and readers. They also simplify "6 + 2" to the constant 8. The differences are purely stylistic: query id 1 uses short table aliases (ss, sr, cs, d1, d2, d3, s, i), making the join conditions and select list more readable and less error-prone, while preserving identical logic, grouping, ordering, and limits. Since both have identical performance characteristics, readability and maintainability favor id 1.

       min(ss.ss_net_profit) as store_sales_profit,
       min(sr.sr_net_loss) as store_returns_loss,
       min(cs.cs_net_profit) as catalog_sales_profit
from store_sales ss
join store_returns sr
  on ss.ss_customer_sk = sr.sr_customer_sk
 and ss.ss_item_sk = sr.sr_item_sk
 and ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
join catalog_sales cs
  on sr.sr_customer_sk = cs.cs_bill_customer_sk
 and sr.sr_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
join date_dim d1 on d1.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
join date_dim d2 on d2.d_date_sk = sr.sr_returned_date_sk
join date_dim d3 on d3.d_date_sk = cs.cs_sold_date_sk
join store s on s.s_store_sk = ss.ss_store_sk

The rewritten and enhanced plans introduce table aliases (e.g., `store_returns sr`, `store_sales ss`, `item i`, `store s`, `catalog_sales cs`), but the optimizer selected the same physical operators, join orders, index usage, and parallelism strategy in all three cases. The only measurable difference is a trivial cost change at the Limit/GroupAggregate boundary: the original estimated the upper bound at 35253.68, while both rewritten and enhanced plans estimate 35253.67 — a difference of approximately 0.0000003% (effectively noise from cost rounding). All node-level costs, row estimates, and widths are otherwise unchanged.

1. Cost Efficiency:
- Overall cost change: The total plan cost remains essentially unchanged across all three versions — approximately 35241.42 (startup) to ~35253.67/35253.68 (total). The overall cost change is effectively 0% (a nominal decrease of 0.01 cost units, roughly 0.00003%, in the rewritten/enhanced plans).
- Cost at the most expensive plan node: The most expensive node is the innermost Nested Loop feeding the Sort (cost=1.60..34229.99), which is identical in all three plans. No cost reduction was achieved at this dominant node.

2. Plan Characteristics:
- Scan type transitions: None. All three plans use the same access methods: Parallel Index Scan on date_dim (d2), Index Scans on store_returns, store_sales (primary key), store, item, and date_dim (d1, d3), plus a Bitmap Heap Scan on catalog_sales supported by BitmapAnd over two Bitmap Index Scans. The rewrite did not enable any Seq Scan → Index Scan improvements.
- Join algorithm refinements: None. The plan retains the same five Nested Loop joins, the Gather Merge for parallel coordination, and the BitmapAnd-based semi-join to catalog_sales in all versions.
- Elimination of explicit sorting / intermediate result reduction: Not achieved. The Sort node (Sort Key: item_id, item_desc, store_id, store_name) remains in all plans, and row estimates (rows=1 at nearly every upper node, rows=3650/3126 at the store_returns fan-out) are unchanged, indicating no reduction in intermediate result sets.

3. Resource Utilization:
- Memory usage: No change. There are no Hash nodes in any plan (no Hash Join / Hash Aggregate was chosen or removed), and Sort memory expectations are identical. Buffer usage patterns implied by the plan are unchanged.
- Worker count: Unchanged. All three plans plan 2 parallel workers for the date_dim Parallel Index Scan and the subsequent Gather Merge. No adjustment was made or was needed.

4. Other Improvements:
- Readability: The rewritten and enhanced plans introduce explicit table aliases (sr, ss, i, s, cs, d1–d3), which improve the readability of join conditions and filter predicates, though this is a cosmetic improvement only and does not affect execution.
- Optimization opportunity: The plan is dominated by the deep Nested Loop chain (cost ≈ 34230) driven by the store_returns → store_sales join with ~3126 rows fanned out per date. The estimated rows=1 at nearly every upper node suggests potential cardinality misestimation; a rewrite that introduces Hash Join or pre-aggregation at the store_returns/store_sales stage, or improved statistics/indexes on the (sr_item_sk, sr_ticket_number, sr_customer_sk) join path, could yield meaningful gains. However, the current rewrite/enhancement did not alter the plan shape, so no such benefit was realized.

Conclusion: The rewrite and enhancement produced functionally identical plans with only cosmetic aliasing changes and negligible cost differences. Further optimization should target the dominant Nested Loop / Sort subtree and cardinality estimation rather than syntax-level rewriting.
